#10632. Scientists recreate a bizarre quantum effect that stops a particle cold

A particle does not have to pass through a magnetic field to be influenced by it. That surprising feature of quantum physics has now been recreated by researchers at the University of Oxford, who used a hybrid quantum computer to demonstrate how an invisible magnetic influence can bring a simulated particle's movement to a complete stop.

The experiment reproduced a phenomenon known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect, in which a charged particle traveling around a region containing magnetic flux experiences a measurable change in its quantum state, even though its path never takes it through the magnetic field itself.

The Oxford team achieved this using a quantum system that combines qubits, the basic units of quantum information, with quantum oscillators, which represent vibrating motion. Their findings, published in Nature Physics, demonstrate how this combination could help scientists investigate fundamental interactions between matter and fields that are increasingly difficult to simulate using conventional computers.

A Strange Quantum Effect Predicted in 1959

In everyday physics, a charged particle responds to the electric and magnetic fields it encounters. Quantum mechanics, however, reveals that particles can behave in ways that have no straightforward counterpart in our ordinary experience.

In 1959, physicists Yakir Aharonov and David Bohm predicted that a charged particle could be affected by magnetic flux even if it traveled entirely outside the region containing the magnetic field. Rather than experiencing a conventional magnetic force along its path, the particle would undergo a change in its quantum phase.

Quantum phase describes an aspect of a particle's wave behavior that influences how different possible paths interfere with one another. Although the phase itself is not directly visible, differences in phase can produce measurable effects.

The prediction became known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect and was eventually verified in experiments involving real electrons. It remains one of the clearest demonstrations that the quantum world operates according to rules that go beyond the familiar laws of classical physics.

Researchers are now exploring how this phenomenon behaves in more complex theoretical systems known as lattice gauge theories.

These theories describe how matter interacts with fields associated with fundamental forces. They represent space as a grid, with matter located at individual points and fields associated with the connections between them. Such models are particularly important in particle physics and high-energy physics, where scientists study the basic particles and interactions that make up the universe.

The challenge is that the mathematics becomes extraordinarily demanding as these systems grow more complicated. Even powerful conventional computers can struggle to calculate how all the interacting components evolve.

Quantum computers offer a different approach. Instead of attempting to calculate every aspect of a quantum system using classical computing methods, scientists can construct another quantum system that follows the same underlying rules. They can then observe its behavior directly, an approach known as quantum simulation.

How Scientists Built the Quantum Experiment

The Oxford project began in 2022, when lead author Dr. Sebastian Saner, Dr. Oana Bazavan, and their colleagues started developing a way to simulate lattice gauge theories using a hybrid quantum system. They collaborated with Dr. Alejandro Bermudez of the Instituto de Física Teórica in Madrid.

Their experimental design relied on two different quantum components that performed separate functions.

The first consisted of qubits encoded in the internal states of trapped ions, which are electrically charged atoms held in place using electromagnetic fields. These qubits represented the gauge fields in the theoretical model.

The second component used quantum oscillators, created by the vibrations of those same trapped ions. These oscillators represented matter.

By combining the two, the researchers constructed a simple loop that served as a basic building block of a lattice gauge theory. Two oscillators represented matter at two different locations, while two qubits represented the fields connecting those locations.

Although the arrangement was small, it was sufficient to reproduce an important interaction between matter and magnetic flux.

Quantum Entanglement Creates a Surprising Solution

The researchers next placed the two qubits into an entangled quantum state. Entanglement is a phenomenon in which quantum systems become linked in ways that cannot be fully explained by classical physics.

Within the team's simulated lattice gauge theory, this entangled state represented magnetic flux passing through the center of the loop.

Initially, the researchers had planned to introduce the magnetic flux as a fixed background condition. However, creating the necessary interaction would have required capabilities that their quantum hardware did not possess.

Encoding the flux directly into the qubits provided a practical alternative. More importantly, it allowed the magnetic flux to become part of the evolving quantum system rather than remaining an unchanging external influence.

"For us, the exciting step was to encode the magnetic flux in a gauge field that was itself dynamical," says Dr. Saner. "Rather than having matter evolve in a fixed background, the matter and gauge field become part of the same quantum dynamics."

What began as a technical workaround ultimately enabled the researchers to explore a more interesting version of the phenomenon, one in which matter and the field could participate together in the quantum dynamics.

A Quantum Effect That Stops Particle Motion

With their simulated loop in place, the scientists examined how a matter particle moved between locations through a process known as quantum tunneling.

Quantum tunneling allows particles to move between states in ways that would not be possible under ordinary classical rules.

When the researchers ran their simulation without magnetic flux, the particle could tunnel freely around the loop.

Introducing the simulated flux produced a strikingly different result.

The particle had two possible paths around the loop, but the magnetic flux changed their relative quantum phases. As a result, the two paths interfered destructively, meaning their quantum contributions canceled each other out.

This interference completely suppressed the tunneling, leaving the simulated particle frozen in its original state.

The outcome demonstrated the Aharonov-Bohm effect within a lattice gauge theory containing a dynamical field. Rather than simply reproducing the familiar behavior of a particle moving around a fixed magnetic influence, the experiment showed how the phenomenon can emerge when matter and the field are both represented by interacting quantum components.

A New Tool for Exploring Fundamental Physics

The Oxford findings were published alongside a related study by researchers at the University of Maryland, led by Professor Norbert Linke.

The Maryland team also used a hybrid quantum system, but their experiment focused on simulating the Yukawa potential, a mathematical description of an interaction relevant to nuclear and particle physics.

Although the two research groups developed their methods independently, they later coordinated the submission of their studies to Nature Physics.

Together, the experiments demonstrate how hybrid quantum computers could become valuable tools for investigating fundamental interactions that are difficult to reproduce using classical simulations.

By combining different types of quantum components, researchers can build systems in which matter and fields are represented in ways that more closely reflect the physics they want to study.

The Oxford experiment provides an example of that potential. A phenomenon first predicted in 1959 and later observed with electrons has now been recreated in a quantum simulator where the field itself participates in the dynamics, offering scientists another way to explore the unusual rules governing the quantum world.

Materials provided by University of Oxford. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10631. Best Static Website Hosting Providers in 2027: Free and Paid Options Compared

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You've finished building your website. The HTML, CSS, and JavaScript files are ready, everything works locally, and now you need somewhere to publish it.

Static website hosting is one of the simplest ways to get a website online. Many providers offer free plans, and you don't need to maintain a traditional web server.

The harder part is choosing the right provider. Some let you upload a single HTML file and share it immediately. Others connect to GitHub, automatically deploy code changes, and support entire development teams.

We've compared eight static hosting providers, including TiinyHost, Cloudflare Pages, Netlify, Vercel, and GitHub Pages. Below, you'll find their free hosting limits, pricing, advantages, and drawbacks, along with recommendations for different types of projects.

Static website hosting is a service that stores and delivers pre-built website files, including HTML, CSS, JavaScript, images, and fonts.

Unlike traditional server-rendered websites, static sites don't need a server to generate each page when someone visits. The HTML is already prepared and can be delivered directly to the visitor's browser.

That doesn't mean static websites can't be interactive. JavaScript can still handle navigation, forms, filtering, and other features. Static sites can also communicate with external APIs or databases.

The main distinction is how pages are generated.

You can write a static website using plain HTML or generate one with frameworks and tools such as Astro, Hugo, Eleventy, or Next.js static export.

Static hosting is also commonly associated with Jamstack, an approach that separates the front end from back-end services.

Lower hosting costs: Many providers let you publish smaller websites for free.

Simpler maintenance: There's no application server or server-side runtime to maintain for the static pages themselves.

Efficient content delivery: Static files can be cached across a content delivery network (CDN), reducing the distance between visitors and website assets.

There are limitations, too. If your website requires user authentication, database operations, or server-side functionality, you'll need additional services or a hosting platform that supports them.

We compared eight providers based on their deployment methods, free plan allowances, pricing, and suitability for different projects.

Free hosting limits: Bandwidth, storage, build allowances, and restrictions.

Deployment process: Whether you can upload files directly or need a Git repository.

Custom domains and HTTPS: Support for publishing under your own domain.

Development features: Automatic deployments, preview environments, and integrations.

Pricing: What happens when a project exceeds its free allowance.

Usage restrictions: Whether free hosting is suitable for commercial projects.

The following table summarizes the main differences between all eight services.

Free plans aren't directly comparable. For example, Vercel restricts its Hobby plan to personal, non-commercial use, while GitHub Pages prohibits using the service as free hosting for an online business or commercial SaaS.

Bandwidth limits also aren't the same as visitor limits. A website serving large images or downloadable files can consume its monthly allowance with relatively few visitors.

Cloudflare Pages is a strong choice for developers who want to host static websites without worrying about a conventional monthly bandwidth cap.

You can connect a GitHub or GitLab repository, and Cloudflare automatically builds and deploys the website when you push changes. Direct uploads are also supported.

A maximum individual file size of 25 MiB.

The combination is useful for blogs, documentation websites, and other projects that primarily serve HTML, CSS, JavaScript, and images.

Cloudflare also provides preview deployments, custom domains, and HTTPS support.

No fixed bandwidth cap for static asset delivery.

Generous free plan for typical static websites.

Monthly build limits can matter for frequently updated projects.

Cloudflare's product range can make the initial setup less straightforward for beginners.

Cloudflare is increasingly emphasizing Workers Static Assets for new projects. Pages remains available, but developers starting a new application should compare both deployment approaches.

For a conventional static website, however, Pages is still a practical option, particularly when traffic could grow beyond the limits of other free plans.

See the official Cloudflare Pages limits for the current allowances.

Netlify combines static website hosting with tools designed to make development and deployment easier.

Connect a Git repository, configure your build settings, and Netlify can publish a new version whenever you push changes.

It also creates deploy previews, allowing you to inspect changes before merging them into your production branch.

This is useful when multiple people work on the same website or when you want to review design changes before publishing.

Netlify supports custom domains, HTTPS, serverless functions, and form handling.

Netlify's free plan uses a credit-based model with 300 credits per month.

Production deployments consume 15 credits each.

Web requests consume 2 credits per 10,000 requests.

Deploy previews and branch deployments consume no deployment credits.

The paid Personal plan starts at $9 per month and includes 1,000 monthly credits.

Convenient Git integration and deploy previews.

Built-in features such as forms and serverless functions.

Suitable for both simple websites and more advanced front-end projects.

Credit-based pricing requires monitoring several types of usage.

A busy site with frequent deployments can consume its free allowance faster than expected.

When comparing Netlify alternatives, Cloudflare Pages is particularly worth considering if your main concern is bandwidth.

For developers choosing between Vercel vs Netlify, the deciding factor is often the framework and workflow. Netlify offers a flexible approach to static site deployment, while Vercel has particularly close integration with Next.js.

Vercel is particularly well suited to Next.js applications, although it also supports React, Vue, Svelte, Astro, and other frameworks.

Like Netlify, Vercel connects to your Git repository and deploys updates automatically.

Every deployment receives its own URL, which helps developers review changes before publishing them to the main website.

Vercel also supports server-side rendering, functions, and other features beyond traditional static site hosting.

There is one important restriction: Vercel Hobby is intended for personal, non-commercial use only.

If you're deploying a business website or another commercial project, you'll need an appropriate paid plan.

Convenient deployment workflow for Next.js.

Supports both static and server-rendered applications.

Paid usage can involve multiple resource-based charges.

If you're building a personal Next.js application, Vercel is an obvious candidate. For a basic HTML website, however, its additional framework features may be unnecessary.

GitHub Pages is a straightforward way to publish a website directly from a GitHub repository.

It works particularly well for developer portfolios, project documentation, open-source websites, and small informational sites.

You can upload HTML, CSS, and JavaScript files to a repository, configure GitHub Pages, and publish them under a github.io subdomain.

GitHub Pages can serve existing static files or build websites using supported workflows, including Jekyll and GitHub Actions.

Published websites have a maximum size of 1 GB.

Bandwidth has a soft limit of 100 GB per month.

Free accounts can publish Pages sites from public repositories.

The bandwidth and build restrictions are soft limits, meaning they aren't necessarily enforced as strict cutoffs.

Simple integration with GitHub repositories.

Well suited to documentation and open-source projects.

Free accounts require a public repository for Pages.

GitHub Pages is not intended for running online businesses, e-commerce websites, or commercial SaaS products.

GitHub Pages is particularly convenient if your code already lives on GitHub and you only need to publish documentation or a portfolio.

For commercial websites, Cloudflare Pages or Netlify may be more appropriate.

The GitHub Pages documentation explains its hosting limits and usage restrictions.

Not every website needs a repository, deployment pipeline, or command-line interface.

If you've created a simple HTML page and want to share it, TiinyHost lets you upload an HTML file or ZIP archive and publish it under a shareable URL.

It can also host PDFs, making it useful for sharing documents and presentations alongside website projects.

The main advantage is the upload process. You don't need to configure a repository or install development tools before publishing.

Sharing an HTML file with someone who doesn't use Git.

The free plan includes one active site, a 3 MB project upload limit, and an allowance of 5,000 monthly visits.

The Tiny plan costs €5 per month when billed monthly. It supports uploads up to 25 MB and includes built-in analytics.

The Solo plan starts at €13 per month when billed monthly and adds features such as custom domains, password protection, and additional projects.

Annual billing is available at lower effective monthly prices.

No Git repository or command line required.

Suitable for previews and smaller projects.

The free plan's 3 MB limit is restrictive for websites with many images or other assets.

Custom domains require the Solo plan or higher.

Upload-based publishing is less convenient than Git automation for frequently updated websites.

For someone who simply needs free HTML hosting to share a small project, TiinyHost is worth considering.

For a larger production website that receives regular updates, Git-based hosting is likely to be more manageable.

Firebase Hosting is Google's hosting service for static websites and web application assets.

It integrates with other Firebase products, including Authentication, Cloud Firestore, and Cloud Functions.

That makes it useful when a website needs static hosting but also relies on Google's back-end services.

Deployment is handled through the Firebase command-line interface.

After configuring your project, you can deploy using:

Firebase distributes website assets through its CDN and supports custom domains with SSL certificates.

The Blaze plan uses pay-as-you-go billing while retaining applicable no-cost allowances.

One detail worth checking is the distinction between Firebase Hosting and Firebase App Hosting. They are separate products with different features and pricing models.

Integrates with Firebase Authentication and Firestore.

Useful for applications already using Google's developer services.

Initial setup is more involved than uploading an HTML file.

The daily transfer allowance may become restrictive for larger websites.

Firebase Hosting makes the most sense when Firebase is already part of your application.

For a standalone static blog or portfolio that doesn't use Firebase services, Cloudflare Pages or GitHub Pages may be simpler.

Amazon S3 and CloudFront offer a different approach to static hosting.

Instead of using a platform that handles the entire deployment process, you combine AWS services to store and deliver your website.

S3 stores the website files, while CloudFront distributes them through a CDN.

This setup provides control over caching, permissions, security, and integration with other AWS services.

It can be suitable for organizations already using AWS or developers who need specific infrastructure configurations.

AWS uses usage-based pricing for S3 storage, requests, and applicable CloudFront usage.

Free allowances may apply depending on your account and the current AWS pricing program, but you should calculate costs based on your expected traffic, storage, and requests.

Unlike simpler hosting services, there isn't one fixed monthly price for every website.

Several services can contribute to the final bill.

Incorrect permissions or origin settings can cause access errors.

One common issue when setting up S3 static website hosting is receiving a 403 Forbidden error because of bucket permissions or CloudFront origin configuration.

AWS recommends using CloudFront Origin Access Control with a regular S3 bucket origin so that the bucket can remain private. This differs from exposing the S3 website endpoint directly, which does not support HTTPS on its own.

AWS S3 and CloudFront are worth considering if you need that level of control. For a small static website, a managed hosting platform usually requires less setup.

Render is a cloud platform that supports static websites alongside web services, databases, background workers, and other application components.

Its static hosting works through a Git repository.

Connect your repository, select the build command and publish directory, and Render automatically deploys your website.

The service includes CDN delivery, custom domains, and managed TLS certificates.

Render is particularly useful when your project includes both a static front end and back-end services that you want to manage through the same platform.

Render's Hobby workspace is free and includes:

500 standard build pipeline minutes per month.

Additional bandwidth is billed at $0.15 per GB under the current pricing structure, so the Hobby workspace should not be treated as unlimited free hosting.

The Pro workspace costs $25 per month, excluding any applicable usage-based compute charges.

Static websites and back-end services on one platform.

The 5 GB included bandwidth allowance is relatively small.

Render makes sense when you're already using it for other parts of an application.

If you only need to host a static HTML website, Cloudflare Pages offers a more generous bandwidth allowance without the additional platform complexity.

Free static website hosting can be sufficient for a personal portfolio, documentation site, prototype, or small informational website.

However, the restrictions vary considerably between providers.

Some hosts measure data transfer, while others use visitor counts or a credit system. Check how each provider calculates usage rather than comparing headline numbers alone.

If your website rebuilds whenever you publish content, deployment limits matter. A blog updated a few times a month has different requirements from an application receiving dozens of deployments every day.

Free hosting doesn't always mean you can run a business website. Vercel's Hobby plan and GitHub Pages have restrictions that may rule out certain commercial projects.

Some services include custom domains on free plans. Others require an upgrade before you can connect one.

A free plan might stop serving additional usage, while another provider may bill for overages.

Before publishing a production website, check the provider's billing behavior and configure spending controls where available.

The right service depends primarily on how you build your website and what you intend to do with it.

For most developers publishing a conventional static website, Cloudflare Pages is a reasonable starting point.

If you're working with Git and want convenient previews and deployment tools, Netlify is another good choice.

For small files, temporary demos, and client previews, a simpler upload-based service may save time.

You should also consider how often the website will change.

Uploading files manually may be fine for a page that rarely changes. For a website with regular content updates, automated deployments from Git will generally be easier to maintain.

Publishing your website is only the first step. If you want search engines to discover and understand your pages, check the technical SEO basics before sharing the URL.

Every important page should have a descriptive HTML title and a relevant meta description.

You can use the Meta Tags Generator to create the necessary markup.

Open Graph tags help control how a page appears when shared on platforms that support them.

Set the title, description, canonical page URL where appropriate, and preview image.

A robots.txt file tells compliant crawlers which paths they may access. It doesn't guarantee indexing or prevent a public page from appearing in search results.

Use the Robots.txt Generator to create your crawler rules.

Include a sitemap with the canonical URLs you want search engines to discover.

Structured data helps search engines interpret eligible content.

Depending on the website, you may want Organization, Article, BreadcrumbList, or other relevant schema types.

The JSON-LD Schema Generator can help generate markup.

Confirm the correct domain loads over HTTPS.

Check canonical tags and redirect behavior.

Make sure important pages don't contain unintended noindex directives.

Test page performance and Core Web Vitals.

Verify the website in Google Search Console.

Static hosting makes serving website files relatively straightforward, but technical SEO still requires configuration and testing.

Cloudflare Pages is a good general-purpose choice because of its free static asset bandwidth and Git integration. Netlify is well suited to teams that need deploy previews, while GitHub Pages works well for portfolios and documentation. The best option depends on your deployment workflow, traffic requirements, and whether the website is commercial.

Yes. TiinyHost, Cloudflare Pages, Netlify, GitHub Pages, Firebase Hosting, Render, and several other services offer free hosting options. Most free plans have restrictions involving bandwidth, deployments, storage, or usage. Check the terms before using a free plan for a business website.

A small static website can cost nothing to host if it stays within a provider's free allowances. Paid plans vary from fixed monthly subscriptions to usage-based pricing. Domain registration, paid back-end services, and additional storage or bandwidth may increase the total cost.

For a standalone HTML file, upload-based hosting is generally the simplest approach. Services such as TiinyHost let you publish a file without configuring Git or a build process. If the project already lives in a GitHub repository, GitHub Pages or Cloudflare Pages may be more convenient.

GitHub Pages is a sensible option for documentation, open-source projects, and personal portfolios. Netlify offers more deployment features, including preview environments, forms, and functions. It also supports commercial projects, making it more appropriate for some business websites that would fall outside GitHub Pages' permitted uses.

Static website hosting doesn't need to be expensive or complicated. The important part is choosing a service that matches your website and deployment process.

Cloudflare Pages is a good starting point for static websites that need generous free bandwidth. Netlify is useful for Git-based development, while GitHub Pages suits personal projects and documentation.

If you're publishing a small HTML file or sharing a prototype, upload-based hosting may be all you need.

#10629. Ex-police officer becomes VTuber to help stop viewers from committing crimes

A former Japanese police officer has become a VTuber, promising to share his experiences in patrol cars and riot police units to teach viewers about the law.

VTubers in Japan aren’t exactly strangers when it comes to collaborating with real-life law enforcement and government awareness campaigns. After all, Hololive’s AZKi was famously appointed a traffic regulation adviser and police station chief for a day by Kagawa Prefectural Police in March 2026.

However, it’s rarer for former police officers to leave their crime-fighting days behind and debut as a virtual creator. Well, that’s exactly what VTuber Sukenosuke Hongo has done, and the officer-turned-anime boy aims to share his practical advice with his viewers.

In his introduction video, which has garnered a whopping one million views since November 1, the former officer said he wants to teach his viewers about the law and how to protect themselves.

During his debut announcement, Sukenosuke singled out drivers as one group he hopes to help, explaining that his time responding to emergency calls showed him how often people struggle to handle these situations.

“When I responded to emergency calls and listened to people explain what happened, there were honestly plenty of times when I thought, ‘What is this person talking about?’” he said. “That’s why I want to teach people the correct information.”

Sukenosuke also aims to stop his viewers from becoming victims of crime as well, using his real-life experience working in “police boxes, patrol cars, and in riot police units” to keep fans more informed.

“The fundamental purpose of crime prevention is to stop you from becoming a victim in the first place,” said the VTuber. “Even if that next incident does happen, knowing how to respond properly makes it easier for the police to take action, too.”

#10628. The European Commission's new Digital Fairness Act could change monetisation in games forever - and not just in Europe

"Well, we'll just do this for the rest of the world."

The European Commission has published its proposed Digital Fairness Act, which has the potential to change how video games are monetised - worldwide - forever. A draft of the Act was leaked yesterday and what's been published today appears to be, to my eye, the same, though there's now also a mini-site accompanying it that explores the Act in more detail and uses more understandable language.

And note that while the Digital Fairness Act isn't about video games exclusively - it's much broader than that - it does mention video games specifically, highlighting both their increased importance to society now, and that policy-shaping bodies like the European Commission are prepared to take them seriously.

The Digital Fairness Act is set to become European law, probably sometime between 2028 and 2029, once everyone's discussed it and made whatever amendments they're going to make to it. And at that point, breaching the regulations will come with potentially steep fines (up to six percent of a company's worldwide annual turnover, or up to €2m where an annual turnover isn't available).

The biggest things in the Digital Fairness Act relating to video games are regulations around loot boxes (sometimes referred to as "variable reward systems" or systems with "uncertainty-based rewards") and virtual currencies, particularly with regard to "minors" - anyone under 18 years old.

The Act stipulates paid-for virtual currencies must be disabled in a game by default for minors, then activated by parental and guardian approval, and that paid loot boxes "shall not" be offered to minors at all. Moreover, games must provide a way for everyone to disable paid loot boxes completely, and clearly show the probability of receiving items from them.

It's these changes that have the potential to overhaul how loot boxes work everywhere going forwards, and whether companies use them at all. To understand why, I talked to games business expert George Osborn, who runs the excellent Video Games Industry Memo newsletter, and who's been following the Act closely.

Osborn sees this as a "really big" regulatory change and believes that companies like EA, Xbox, Activision Blizzard, PlayStation, and other big gaming companies, both in console gaming and mobile gaming, will be "really concerned" by this. That's partly because a lot of these things being outlined are not necessarily easy to implement.

For starters, a company now needs a way to work out who's a minor playing its game and who isn't, and it needs to provide "a master switch", as Osborn calls it, for people to switch paid loot boxes off with. These things require not just design changes but perhaps entire alterations to a game's approach; it means theoretically being able to turn paid card packs off in EA Sports FC Ultimate Team, example, and their not being able to be sold to minors at all.

"EA might be looking at it and going, 'This isn't great news for [EA Sports FC] Ultimate Team because people can switch it off,'" Osborn added, "but Ultimate Team is at least hived off in a box." EA could potentially age-gate Ultimate team as a mode and deal with it that way. But what if you're a card game where random paid rewards are central to your premise? "What that now means is you're possibly going to have to find a way to just completely turn that off for users, and it is very difficult to do that when they're embedded into the design of the game."

The virtual currency issue is potentially easier to tackle because existing parental controls can control spending, so those tools could expand to cover it. But another proposal brought forward in the Digital Fairness Act states that a game must clearly show, in a region's local currency, how much an item costs when bought with virtual currency, which adds another layer of complication when considering different currencies and people moving between countries with their devices.

These changes require investment and thought, and they sit alongside broader changes that affect things like subscription services, such as Xbox Live and PlayStation Plus and Nintendo Online, to make them easier to cancel and less able to obfuscate prices and hidden extras. There's even a stipulation that people be able to talk to a human in customer service rather than a chat bot. There are more technicalities for game-making companies to consider.

But why should people outside of the European Union care? These rules are only enforceable there. Because: if a company is selling a game into the European Union, which I expect every game company will want to do - "you don't just want to rule out a market of a few hundred million consumers; it's not a light thing to cut that out", Osborn says - then it will have to comply by these rules. And if it does, how many different versions of a game will it be prepared to make?

Think about the recent global move towards all devices using USB-C chargers: that was a movement started by a European Commission ruling. Think of the new Switch 2s with removable battery packs: that was another change instigated by the European Commission. "Because that market is big enough, you design for it," Obsorn says, "and then your logic becomes, 'Well, we'll just do this for the rest of the world.' You're not going to produce one with the battery sealed in and the battery without. You're just going to find one way to produce things.

"That's likely to be the biggest impact in the UK and the rest of the world," he adds, "as people adjust the designs of their games to follow these rules. There's going to be a strong likelihood that some of the design features will remain within the games because it's easiest to work off a single universal version."

The other thing, which Osborn thinks is more important, is that policy makers around the world are "desperate" for solutions to problems and don't know where to look for them. "So if someone comes up with an answer to a question that's been annoying everybody, like 'what do we do about loot boxes?'," he says, "and it provides a particularly strong - or perceived to be effective - solution, it'll be copied. And it'll be copied because a big regulatory bloc like the European Union, which is known for protecting consumers particularly well, has decided this is a good thing. People trust it."

Then, because companies have already developed a way to comply with those rules, it becomes difficult for them to argue that they can't do it elsewhere. "Because those regulators go, 'Well why not?'" Osborn says. "Because if you build the solution in one place, you can build it elsewhere."

Whether or not this will be the end of loot boxes as we know them, Osborn isn't sure. "My guess would be this will continue to push loot boxes into the fringes of video game monetisation. It was already starting to happen," he says. "I know some really high-profile games have them very prominently, which makes people feel like is there really any change here? But I know a lot of companies behind the scenes started to quietly move away from them in the last few years when they were a reputational problem. If you move to a world in which you're meant to have a massive off-switch in your game for them, that's a world in which fewer companies will go in that direction."

Some will stick with it. "If you're an EA, it's going to be very difficult to move away from the model that has supported your business for years," Osborn says. And card-based deck-building games will struggle to avoid it. "But overall, we're going to see a shift away from it," he adds. "And more broadly what this marks is a much wider crackdown on the way that video games monetise their players. As you start to see that crackdown come into force, game developers are going to have to think much more carefully about how the design decisions they make don't just interact with ethical practices, but actually with legal practices too."

#10626. Introducing three weeks of specialist GTA - and not-GTA! - programming on Eurogamer

Love, hate, or not really care about Grand Theft Auto? We've got something for you.

Hello! This idea started as a single theme week, which we thought we might run in the lead up to Grand Theft Auto 6's launch. Then it turned into a pair of theme weeks. Then we realised we had so much stuff - and that, frankly, GTA 6 would be so dominating it'll be around for far more than a week itself - that we couldn't really fit it into two weeks either.

So, allow me to introduce a whopping three-and-a-bit-ish weeks of specialist GTA- and not-GTA-themed coverage on Eurogamer! (Insert celebratory car horn toots here.)

With GTA 6 launching next Thursday, we'll start with a week of coverage dedicated to the series, beginning today with a reader competition (more on that shortly!) and running until next weekend. We'll be covering GTA from all angles here - retrospectives, features, launch day coverage and beyond - across the series as a whole, as well as the big one we've got coming up. You can see that in one place at our GTA Week hub, which will populate as stories go live.

However! If that - coupled with the inevitable wave of coverage that already comes from an enormous moment in video games like this - sounds like the last thing you want from us, well frankly, we get it. Matching big games with big coverage is a part of the brief at a site like Eurogamer, but it's also far from the only thing we want to be about. And we know it's far from the only thing you're going to want to read about for the rest of November, either.

To counteract that, then: we're also running a solid two weeks of counterprogramming, beginning on Monday 23rd November after GTA 6's launch. Expect all things not-GTA: coverage of lesser-known games, smaller-budget efforts, in-depth features on games and stories that are thematically opposed to it, have nothing to do with it, or take it for inspiration but go in an entirely different direction. All of our special counterprogramming can be found at our Not-GTA hub here as it goes live.

Those of you who've been coming back to Eurogamer for a few years might remember our "Games That Got Away" theme week from a few years back - we're bringing that back in a modified form here, too. Look forward to dedicated write ups - some labelled as reviews, some as broader reflective essays and criticism - on some of the great games we haven't quite found the time for so far this year, as we look to clear our own pile of shame.

Amongst all this, rest assured that normal service is also continuing here as ever. All the essential reporting, criticism, guides and the rest - across all things video games, whether or not that includes the Big One - will carry on as usual.

Ultimately, our hope is to offer something for everyone with this period - both the people who look to us to be comprehensive across gaming's biggest moments, and those who see us as a place for deep, nuanced, or simply fun stories on the medium's lesser-known corners.

Thank you as ever for reading - we really do appreciate it. And on that note, here's a link to Bertie's blog on our readers' competition, where you can win yourself your very own copy of GTA 6.

#10625. KFC is hosting a fake funeral so GTA 6 fans can take the day off

A KFC in Australia is hosting a funeral for a fake person, just so customers have a valid reason to take the day off work and play GTA 6.

With November 19 now just days away, the launch of Grand Theft Auto 6 is finally in reach. In a matter of hours, New Zealand-based players will be among the first to jump into Leonida and experience Jason and Lucia’s story.

As a result, plenty of fans have been plotting time off work, just so they don’t have any distractions. In the United States, the game is expected to cause $1 billion in disruption to the economy, with around 1.5 million people estimated to be taking the day off.

Not everyone has the time booked off, however, and some have been looking for other reasons to ditch work. Well, in Australia, one KFC is hosting a funeral, aimed at giving customers a legitimate excuse for taking the day off.

The restaurant, which is based in Robina, Queensland, is celebrating the life of ‘Ann Alibi’, and luckily enough, the ‘ceremony’ will start right as GTA 6 goes live.

“She was always there for us when we needed her. In sickness and in perfect health,” the event invite says. “By remarkable coincidence, her service will take place on the very same day something 13 years in the making finally arrives.”

KFC also added that “the service will include a hearse, coffin, floral tributes, a book of condolences, an order of service and even a heartfelt eulogy delivered by a surprise guest.”

#10624. Physicists discover “astonishing” property of light that defies expectations

Scientists have discovered a surprising ability of light that allows it to develop spinning patterns as it moves through empty space, without relying on special lenses, mirrors, or materials. The finding reveals that light possesses a hidden geometric structure that can influence its behavior in ways researchers previously thought were difficult to achieve.

The discovery comes from an international team led by researchers at the University of East Anglia (UEA) in the United Kingdom, working with scientists in South Africa. Their experiments demonstrate that carefully prepared light can be controlled through its own internal geometry, offering a new approach to manipulating how light spins and twists.

The findings challenge long-standing assumptions about how different properties of light interact. They could eventually lead to more sensitive medical tests, faster and more secure communications, and advanced quantum technologies that use light to transmit and protect information.

Why the Direction of Light's Spin Matters

One of the most intriguing aspects of the discovery involves chirality, a scientific term describing the difference between left-handed and right-handed structures.

Human hands provide a familiar example. Although they are mirror images of each other, a left hand cannot be perfectly superimposed on a right hand. Many molecules have a similar property, including compounds used in medicines. Two versions of the same molecule can have nearly identical chemical structures yet produce very different effects in the body.

Scientists can distinguish these molecular forms by studying how they interact with light that has a particular rotational behavior. Traditionally, producing and controlling such light has often involved specialized materials, carefully designed surfaces, or powerful lenses that focus beams extremely tightly.

The research demonstrates that appropriately prepared light can develop this behavior naturally as it travels, without requiring those additional optical components.

"Our work shows that light can naturally develop this handed behavior all on its own," said Dr. Kayn Forbes from UEA's School of Chemistry, Pharmacy and Pharmacology.

"You just have to prepare it in the right way.

"Most people think of light as traveling in straight lines. But scientists can also create structured light -- light whose brightness, shape, and direction are carefully arranged.

"One extreme example is light that twists as it travels, forming a corkscrew shape known as an optical vortex. Each twist can carry information, making this kind of light valuable for high-speed internet, secure communications, and advanced sensors.

"Light can also spin as it travels, depending on how it is polarized. This spin can be left-handed or right-handed -- another form of chirality."

Scientists Discover How Light Develops a Hidden Spin

Light can exhibit different kinds of rotational behavior. An optical vortex, for example, has a wave pattern that twists around its direction of travel, much like a corkscrew. Light also has a property known as polarization, which describes how its electric field oscillates. Certain polarization patterns produce a form of rotation called spin.

Physicists have long studied the relationship between these two properties. However, the interactions responsible for the newly observed effect were generally considered extremely weak and difficult to detect without specialized experimental conditions.

The researchers found that the situation changes when a beam of light is prepared in a particular balanced configuration.

Under those conditions, the beam can initially have no spin, yet distinct regions with spinning behavior emerge as it travels through free space. The effect does not require the light to pass through engineered materials or additional optical devices.

"It starts off with no spin at all," explained MSc student Light Mkhumbuza, who carried out key experiments. "But as the beam travels forward, spinning regions appear and separate out -- almost as if the spin was hiding and then revealed itself."

Rather than creating spin through an external optical component, the researchers demonstrated how the beam's existing internal structure can determine where spinning regions develop during propagation.

To explain this unexpected behavior, the researchers turned to topology, a mathematical field concerned with properties that remain unchanged when objects are continuously stretched, bent, or reshaped.

Dr. Isaac Nape, from the University of the Witwatersrand in Johannesburg, South Africa, offered a simple way to understand the concept.

"To explain it, imagine a mug and a doughnut," he said. "You can morph one into the other without tearing it, because they both have one hole. That hole is a topological feature."

The researchers found that structured light possesses its own topological characteristics, encoded in the arrangement of its polarization.

This underlying pattern acts like a mathematical fingerprint. Even as the light beam changes shape while traveling, its topological structure remains, influencing how different regions of the beam evolve.

As a result, the arrangement of the light itself can determine where its left-handed and right-handed spinning patterns become visible.

The discovery gives scientists a way to guide this behavior by designing the beam's initial structure, rather than depending on specialized materials to produce the desired effect.

"This gives us a completely new tuning knob for light. By adjusting its topology, we can decide how and where chirality appears," said Dr. Nape.

Potential Breakthroughs in Medicine and Communications

The ability to control light through its geometry could eventually make several important optical technologies simpler, more affordable, and more versatile.

One particularly promising application involves medical diagnostics and pharmaceutical research. Because many biologically important molecules have left-handed and right-handed forms, carefully structured light could help scientists identify these differences with greater sensitivity. Such tools could be valuable for evaluating drug safety and detecting biological markers associated with disease.

The same principles could support smaller optical sensors capable of identifying chemicals or biological substances without requiring large, sophisticated laboratory instruments.

Communications technology could also benefit. By encoding information into different twisting and spinning patterns, researchers may be able to increase the amount of data carried by individual light beams. This could contribute to faster optical communications, improved information security, and future quantum networks.

Other potential uses include manipulating microscopic particles, cells, and molecules with light, as well as developing more resilient quantum systems. Topological properties may help protect delicate quantum information against certain forms of noise and interference.

"The implications are wide-ranging," said Dr. Forbes. "This work could lead to simpler and more sensitive medical tests, especially in drug development.

"It could also be used to pack more information into laser beams -- boosting data capacity for communications, including future quantum networks.

"And because the effect doesn't rely on fragile materials or precision-engineered surfaces, it could be easier and cheaper to use in real-world technologies.

"This research could lay the foundations for a new generation of light-based technologies, by showing that light's behavior can be controlled using its own internal geometry," he added.

These applications remain possibilities for future development, but the underlying discovery provides researchers with another way to design and control the behavior of light.

A Hidden Property of Light Finally Revealed

For decades, scientists have relied on increasingly sophisticated optical equipment to produce and manipulate unusual forms of light. The findings suggest that some of these behaviors can instead arise from carefully designed structures already encoded within a light beam.

By showing how topology governs the emergence of spinning regions during propagation, the team has revealed a previously underappreciated connection between light's geometry and its physical behavior.

The discovery also illustrates how much remains to be learned about something as familiar and extensively studied as light.

"For something so familiar, light is proving to be far richer, stranger and more powerful than anyone imagined," said Dr. Forbes.

"And astonishingly, this new behavior has been there all along -- just waiting to be seen."

The research, 'Topological Control of Chirality and Spin with Structured Light', was published in the journal Light: Science & Applications.

#10620. What we've been playing - "Whether he means to or not, he serves sass all over town"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Another week has climaxed and I'm so sorry you had to read that on a Saturday morning. But here we are again, with our regular feature, where we talk about what we've been playing in order that you do too. This week, Marie has been staring at monster butts; Connor has capitulated to World of Warcraft; Kelsey is discovering the joys of a beloved Zelda game for the first time; and Bertie enjoys someone being punched in the face, because I guess he's that sort of person?

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago. And now onward!

My favourite thing so far about Ocarina of Time is the judgement plastered on little Link's face when he encounters, well, almost everyone. Whether he means to or not, he serves sass all over town, and in Kakariko Village, and I am very much here for it.

Also, I wasn't prepared to come face-to-face with a monster's butt cheeks. At all. Imagine the scene: I'm enjoying the tunes of the Lost Woods, processing the changes that have happened to Hyrule in the last seven years, all while making my way to the Forest Temple. Then the Moblin's turn their backs to reveal... Well, everything.

This week I've spent an ungodly amount of time playing World of Warcraft: Forever (you may have read my preview on the website) and have been essentially glued to it during every minute of free time I've had away from work. I've got both my main professions above the level 225 mark and I'm firmly in the thick of it now. Levels take longer, items are actually getting good, and I've had to make a second alt character for extra bank space.

As a result, I've not been to the gym in a while. I actually got one of those emails asking why I've fallen off, offering some tips to get back into the routine. Sorry Nuffield Health, my sleep schedule is ruined and I've substituted proper workouts for squats and basic at-home cardio. Surely, this can't go wrong...

I don't have the same level of nostalgia for Ocarina of Time that my peers do, but that has not stopped me from enjoying every minute of running around Hyrule smashing pots and trying not to cry over the touching cutscenes and, well, Saria.

The game feels like Dark Souls for children (endearing) and that is completely my jam. It's magical, satisfying, and often sad. There's creepy enemies as well as cute ones, trap floors and doors, mysterious NPCs... and my heart just feels so warm whenever I'm playing it. My fingers are now crossed that there's a Majora's Mask remake in the pipeline over at Nintendo. (As well as a Super Mario 64 remake, which I admit, is pushing it. But for what it’s worth, I'd probably sell my soul to see it get the Ocarina treatment.)

It takes me a while to settle with games; it takes me a while to settle full stop. I suppose in Fire Emblem's case I'm out of touch, seeing as the last instalment I played was on 3DS. But I struggled with the garish enthusiasm early on and the whole thing seemed overly simple. Nevertheless, I've settled a bit now, and now that the game's layering on more strategy and Intelligent System's expertise is coming to the fore, I'm beginning to enjoy myself much more.

I wasn't expecting it to be quite so boobsy, though. Was Fire Emblem always this way? Perhaps it's my fault for picking the scantily clad queen Theodora, though that sort of presentation isn't reserved only for her. She's no wallflower, though. My favourite moment so far was when she got off her elephant during a parade - yes, her elephant - and walked over to punch a teenage boy in the face for halting proceedings. Apparently she was doing him a favour.

#10619. A Practical Introduction to the Twitch Helix API

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Behind almost every Twitch tool you have ever seen, the stat trackers, the moderation bots, the stream overlays, the alert systems, sits one interface: the Twitch Helix API. Twitch is unusually generous with public data, and Helix is the door to it. If you have ever wondered how third-party sites know who is live, what a channel's viewership looks like, or details a Twitch profile page does not even display, this is the machinery.

This article is a practical overview of Helix for developers who have not used it yet: what it offers, how its authentication works, what people build with it, and where the sharp edges are.

Helix is Twitch's current REST API, served from api.twitch.tv/helix. It replaced the old "Kraken" API, which was fully retired in 2022, so any tutorial still showing Kraken endpoints is a sign to keep scrolling. Helix covers users, streams, channels, games and categories, clips, chat, moderation actions, subscriptions, and events, with JSON in and JSON out.

Two things distinguish it from many platform APIs. First, a large share of it works without any user's permission, because the data is public by Twitch's own definition. Second, it is paired with EventSub, a push system that notifies your application when things happen (a stream goes live, a user follows, a message arrives) instead of forcing you to poll. Most serious Twitch tools use both: Helix for lookups, EventSub for live events.

Authentication is where most newcomers stall, mainly because Twitch offers several OAuth flows and tutorials rarely say which one you actually need. The short version:

Every request needs a Client ID, which you get by registering an application in the Twitch developer console (two-factor authentication on your account is required first). Requests carry the Client ID and a bearer token together; forgetting the Client ID header is the classic mystery 401.

App access tokens come from the client credentials flow, a single server-to-server request with your ID and secret. They have no user attached and that is fine, because public reads, user profiles, live streams, top games, clips, need no user context. For most read-only tools, this is the only flow you ever touch.

Tokens expire, so real applications cache them with their reported lifetime and refresh on a 401 rather than assuming a token lives forever.

A tour of the endpoints that power most tools:

Users. Give the users endpoint a username and it returns the account's public record: display name, profile image, description, Affiliate or Partner status, and the account's exact creation timestamp. That last field is interesting because Twitch does not display it anywhere on a profile, yet it is one of the platform's most useful trust signals, moderators use account age to spot throwaway accounts, and streamers use it to filter giveaway entries. The creation date alone powers a small subcategory of tools: free account age checkers, such as Twitch account age checker, are essentially this one endpoint with a clean page around it, taking any public username and returning the creation date, age, avatar, and broadcaster status. It is a good illustration of how far a single well-chosen Helix field can go.

Streams. The stream's endpoint reports who is live right now, with titles, categories, thumbnail URLs, and viewer counts. This is the backbone of every "is my favorite streamer live" notifier and every directory-style site, and polling it over time is how stat trackers chart viewership history.

Games and categories. Top games, category search, and metadata. Combined with streams data, this is how analytics sites rank categories and how tools answer "how crowded is this category right now."

Clips and videos. Clip metadata, view counts, and creation tools. Highlight compilers and clip-sharing bots live here.

Helix enforces a points-based rate limit per Client ID, and every response tells you where you stand through Ratelimit-Limit, Ratelimit-Remaining, and Ratelimit-Reset headers. Well-behaved tools read those headers and back off before hitting zero; badly behaved ones retry 429s in a loop and wonder why things get worse. For lookup-style tools the practical fix is caching, data like account creation dates never changes, so there is no reason to ask twice.

A few more edges worth knowing before they bite:

Usernames change, IDs do not. Store user IDs, not logins, or your saved data will eventually point at the wrong person.

Missing users are not errors. Looking up a nonexistent username returns a successful response with an empty data array, so handle the empty case explicitly.

Batching exists. The users endpoint takes up to 100 logins in one call, which matters the moment your tool checks lists instead of individuals.

Secrets stay server-side. The client secret identifies your application; calling the token endpoint from browser code hands your app's identity to anyone who opens devtools. Public-facing tools put a small server between the browser and Twitch.

The reason Twitch's third-party ecosystem is so large is that the barrier is so low: one public API, generous free access, and a platform where small questions genuinely matter to people. That is why the space is full of narrow tools that each answer one thing well, when was this account created, is this channel live, how fast is this category growing, which clips popped this week. Most of them are a weekend of work on top of one or two endpoints, and some of them grow into real products.

If you want to start, the path is short: register an application at the developer console, request an app access token, and call the users or streams endpoint for something you actually care about. The official documentation at dev.twitch.tv is current and well maintained, which is not something every platform can claim. Pick one piece of data Twitch collects but does not display, put a clean interface on it, and you are following the same recipe as most of the Twitch tools you already use.

#10617. Taco Bell’s launches new Baja Blast alongside Naked Chicken Chalupa return

Taco Bell is expanding its drink lineup with a new zero-sugar Mountain Dew flavor while preparing to bring back one of its most recognizable menu items.

The fast food chain officially launched Mountain Dew Baja Midnight Zero Sugar nationwide on November 12, offering customers a new version of its passionfruit-flavored fountain drink.

The release comes alongside plans to bring the Naked Chicken Chalupa back to restaurants across the US.

The returning Chalupa, which replaces a traditional shell with crispy chicken, has appeared on Taco Bell’s menu several times since its 2017 debut, including on the chain’s 2016 Decades menu in August 2026.

Rather than using a traditional fried flatbread shell, the chicken itself holds the Chalupa’s fillings, which include shredded lettuce, diced tomatoes, cheddar cheese, and creamy Avocado Ranch Sauce.

The returning menu item will be priced at $5.99, although Taco Bell has not provided a specific nationwide release date in the announcement.

#10616. AI can act exactly like a human, but we still don't think it's conscious

Even when artificial intelligence behaves exactly like a human, people are reluctant to consider it conscious, according to a new study from Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich (LMU).

As artificial intelligence becomes more advanced, reports of AI systems misleading users, working together, or appearing to pursue independent goals are becoming increasingly common. These behaviors often lead people to describe AI as though it can think, feel, or make decisions for itself. But does behaving like a human actually convince people that a machine has consciousness?

New research from LMU suggests that people make a clear distinction between displaying intelligent behavior and possessing a conscious mind, even when humans and AI act in precisely the same way.

How Scientists Tested Perceptions of AI Consciousness

Published in the journal Cognition, the study was led by Dr. Louis Longin of LMU's Chair of Philosophy of Mind, working alongside Dr. Bahador Bahrami, Professor Ophelia Deroy, and other researchers.

"Whereas previous studies have typically asked general questions about whether AI actually has mental states, our study is the first to directly compare how people attribute the same mental states to AI and humans behaving in exactly the same way, under identical circumstances," says Longin.

"We may be using mental terms to refer to AI, but this may only be because we lack better words," says Ophelia Deroy, philosopher and a senior author of the study. "After all, AI is trained to act and speak like a human, so these descriptions seem natural."

To investigate how people judge consciousness, the researchers conducted experiments involving nearly 1,100 participants. One group read brief descriptions of artificial agents that showed different levels of responsiveness to their environments. For example, some agents reacted to sounds or recognized the emotional states of others.

Another group encountered the same situations, except the characters were described as humans rather than AI systems. Participants then evaluated how conscious or aware each character seemed to be of its surroundings.

A Surprising Gap Between Human and AI Consciousness

The experiments revealed that responsiveness influenced how participants judged both humans and artificial intelligence. Characters that reacted more actively to their surroundings were generally considered more conscious and more aware.

However, a striking difference emerged when participants evaluated consciousness specifically. Even the most responsive AI was rated as less conscious than the least responsive human. In other words, identical behavior did not lead people to assign the same level of consciousness to humans and machines.

The results changed considerably when participants were asked about awareness instead. For this characteristic, judgments of humans and AI followed largely similar patterns, suggesting that people interpret awareness and consciousness in fundamentally different ways.

"There is a growing worry in public discussion that people will see AI behaving in a human-like way and start treating these systems as if they had human-like mental states," says Longin, lead author of the study. "We found something much more nuanced: People are quite willing to say that an AI notices things and is aware of its surroundings. But they clearly draw a line when it comes to the term 'consciousness'. People consistently attribute less consciousness to AI than humans -- even when their behavior is identical."

Although people sometimes describe AI as conscious when discussing its actions, the findings indicate that they still distinguish between artificial systems and human minds.

The researchers believe the difference between consciousness and awareness offers an important insight into how people understand artificial intelligence.

Awareness is a relatively neutral term that can describe an agent's ability to detect and respond to information in its environment. Consciousness, however, carries deeper implications about subjective experience and what it means to have an inner mental life.

Because awareness focuses more directly on observable behavior, people appear comfortable applying it to both humans and AI. Consciousness carries a different meaning, leading people to judge the two much less similarly.

The findings also highlight why the language used to discuss artificial intelligence matters, particularly as AI systems become increasingly capable of producing convincing human behavior.

"Our study teaches us lessons for how we communicate about AI," says Bahador Bahrami, who is also a senior author of the study and an expert in social neuroscience. "Companies and journalists often reach for hyped descriptions of AI models, and credit them with intentions, plans, or even moral conscience and hesitation. What our study shows is that everyday judgments are much more discriminating: People do not simply put AI and humans on the same mental scale. But the boundary also depends on which terms we use. For more neutral descriptors such as 'awareness', the distinction can largely disappear. That makes our choice of language important."
